## 2021-07-26

> Op-ed on differential privacy &amp; census data.
> AFAICT approach of census is:
> 1) Take data &amp; add unbiased noise -&gt; get private version that can look weird (e.g. negative counts)
> 2) Post-process to get rid of weirdness -&gt; introduce biases 
> 
> Point is Step 2 is not needed for privacy!

Quoting https://x.com/kinggary/status/1419681466489966596:
> New op-ed by Cynthia Dwork, @ruthgreenwood, and me on a solution to the latest food fight over privacy and the US Census
> https://t.co/FmOt88bPlC

## 2021-03-28

> These are the four most popular misconceptions people have about race &amp; gender bias in algorithms.
> 
> I'm wary of wading into this conversation again, but it's important to acknowledge the research that refutes each point, despite it feeling counter-intuitive.
> 
> Let me clarify.👇🏾

Quoting https://x.com/kareem_carr/status/1375828049720135691:
> FOUR things to know about race and gender bias in algorithms:
> 
> 1. The bias starts in the data
> 
> 2. The algorithms don't create the bias but they do transmit it
> 
> 3. There are a huge number of other biases. Race and gender bias are just the most obvious
> 
> 4. It's fixable! 🧵👇

## 2021-02-12

> @scottlegrand @thomasahle @zzznah No because you contract along the a axis. This generalizes the fact that when you multiply n × k matrix A and a k × m matrix B then you contract along the k-length ax and get an n × m matrix. 
> 
> Nice thing is this automatically handles “extra axes” such as batches, attention heads

https://x.com/boazbaraktcs/status/1360203173336645632 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20210212122527/https://twitter.com/boazbaraktcs/status/1360203173336645632)

## 2021-02-11

> @thomasahle @zzznah If A and B are tensors that share axis a, A⊙ₐ B denotes the tensor  obtained by contracting them along a axis.
> 
> If A and B are vectors with a as their only ax, A⊙ₐ B is their dot product. If A has {a,b} and B has {a,c} then A⊙ₐ B is their matrix product with axes {b,c}

## 2021-01-04 · reply to @boazbaraktcs

> Until the growth slows down.

> If a field is growing exponentially, there will necessarily be many more submissions than qualified people to review them.
> 
> So, reviewing will suck no matter what we do. 
> 
> We can try to make it suck less. More importantly, we can avoid assessing candidates by counting papers.

## 2020-12-28

> @Reza_Zadeh Thanks! Rewriting since I had a typo.
> 
> Proof 1: Slowest car blocks n/2 cars, second slowest among those remaining blocks n/4 etc, to get ~ log n clumps
> 
> Proof 2: # clumps = ∑ Xᵢ where Xᵢ=1 if i-th car from top is slower than cars 1..i-1 . Since Pr[Xᵢ]=1/i, sum is ~ log n.

https://x.com/boazbaraktcs/status/1343364794955554822 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20201228011518/https://twitter.com/boazbaraktcs/status/1343364794955554822)

## 2020-12-28 · possibly deleted

> @Reza_Zadeh Nice! I might borrow it.
> Two proofs:
> Proof 1: Slowest car in expectation blocks ~n/2 cars, slowest among n/2 remaining cars blocks ~n/4 cars, &amp; so on to get ~log n clumps.
> Proof 2: # clumps = ∑ Xᵢ where Xᵢ=1 iff i-th car faster than cars 1..i-1. Happens w.p. 1/i ⇒sum~logn

> This model fits in a tweet and predicts all elections since 1960. 
> 
> def win(A,B):
>    s = ((50*(ord(A[0])-ord(B[0])) + 114*(ord(A[1])-ord(B[1])) 
>        + (ord(A[2])-ord(B[2]))) % 199) % 2
>    return B if s else A https://t.co/XcGJn2Hm5l

> Interesting! Eyeballing @NateSilver538' data https://t.co/0RuuCSfdZj  taking FL as example:

> So donating $40 (roughly!) increases presidential share by one vote on average.

> So what does a 1000-ad advantage in a media market in the last 2 months of the campaign get you?
> 
> In a presidential race, about 2 tenths of a percentage point. 
> 
> In a governor's race? About 4x as much. 
> 
> In a state AG race? 5x as much.
> 
> No surprise: ads matter more down-ballot.
